| CF2 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-195 |
| CF3 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-195 |
| CF4 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-196 |
| CF5 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-198 |
| CF6 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-199 |
| RT1 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-197 |
| RT2 | `unchecked` | — | Not built yet. | GAP-197 |

### 6.8 Two Lifecycles: Platform and Agents
* **L1 – Platform.** Each deployment target (AWS, GCP, Azure, private cloud, …) has its own ops repository, which pins an exact `agent-factory` commit and deploys it through that repository's CI, run manually on its `main`. `agent-factory` knows nothing about any particular deployment. A platform deploy never adds, removes, or changes agents. An ops repository holds no systems, routes, grants, model catalog or secrets (E10, E11, M3, K5).
* **L2 – No agents in the platform repo.** Optional house staff may be shipped as source under `agents/staff`. They are not in the baked catalog and are not agents of a factory until an operator admits them. Test fixtures under `agents/examples` stay equipment, not a fleet.
* **L3 – Admission gate.** An agent owner registers an agent by naming its repository; admission applies to the agent's whole deployment configuration (SK3, SK4): its source and its adopted skills, each at an approved version. A configuration change that alters which skill versions the agent runs (adopting, upgrading, rolling back or removing a skill) goes through admission again and redeploys the agent; a change that leaves the build hash unchanged (a policy or owner change, a pending request) rebuilds nothing. The factory pins the exact commit and runs the equivalent of CI against it: validate `cartridge.yaml`, build the image, run the agent's own tests (a repository without tests is refused), and check the design rules (§6.6, S1, declared secrets and egress). A failure refuses registration with the reasons, and the refusal is ledgered.
* **L4 – Gated deploy.** An agent can deploy only after a policy owner (e.g. FinOps) has set its policy and budget (E7). The deploy itself may be done by the agent's owner, an admin, or the factory under a policy that allows it. The factory deploys exactly the admitted configuration (SK4): an image built from the pinned source and pinned skills, tagged by a build hash of the pinned source and skills, never a mutable tag; a change to the adopted skills is a new configuration version and so a new image and a redeploy (SK4).
* **L5 – New versions.** The factory notices new commits on a registered agent's repository, and changes produced by factory training, and alerts an admin. Nothing is admitted by itself: an admin chooses to admit a commit, and admission (L3) runs on that action. A version that passes shows as "new version available". It goes live only after a person deploys it, the admin or the agent's owner, from the factory (its API and console, never a CI workflow), unless a person has set a policy allowing that agent's admitted versions to deploy automatically (not built: the first build of this flow has no automatic deploy). (Amended 2026-10-07 by Dale, chat: L5 had the factory run every new commit through admission itself; it now alerts and an admin admits. See `docs/registrar-roadmap.md`.)
* **L6 – Agent template.** `agent-factory` provides the template agent builders start from, including a passing test and the admission requirements. It is documentation and scaffolding, not part of any deployed image.
* **Test fixtures** (such as the echo and summarizer cartridges) are equipment for the platform's own tests. They are not agents and never appear in a factory's agent list. A platform deploy's definition of done proves the agent lifecycle by registering a test cartridge through L3–L4, making one gatekeeper-egress-metered call, and retiring it.

### 6.9 Model Access
* **M1 – One factory model API.** Agents call models through the gatekeeper-egress using the factory's model API: the OpenAI Chat Completions request format, documented for agent builders. Agents never call a provider directly and never hold provider credentials (E1, E5, S1).
* **M2 – The owner chooses, and the choice is configuration.** Which model an agent runs on is set by its owner, not by code: the owner selects a model, a backup model, and optionally a model for each skill the agent has adopted, through the API (and the console, which calls it), as often as they like, without rebuilding or redeploying the agent. Cartridges and skills name no model. A model name a call carries is recorded in the ledger but does not decide what serves it. The model served is the owner's selection among the models the policy allows (E7); operations may substitute any offered model without changing the agent. A policy that names no models grants exactly the factory's default model, never every model. The default is factory configuration an admin sets (M3), never a value in code; with none set, a call that needs it is refused (`no_default_model`). **Agents never choose a model** (the user's decision, 2026-10-18): the factory model API serves the run's pin (only if the policy grants it, outside training), else (in training only, where an owner trials models) the requested model, else the policy's first model, else the default, whatever model name the call carries, and records a differing name as `requestedModel`. A policy that lists models grants exactly those; an explicit empty list grants none. An agent still running code that picks a model gets the factory's choice; correcting it is its owner's job. Provider-native routes (M4) stay strict: the model named in a provider's own format is served or refused, never substituted.
* **M3 – Operations decides the offering.** Each factory's offering (provider, the provider's model id, price) is factory data that admins maintain, changed without a redeploy; provider keys are supplied through the Keymaster's platform credentials, and a landing zone supplies only cloud permissions (for example the gatekeeper-egress's access to a cloud model service). Agents never get a route to a model provider other than the gatekeeper-egress's provider routes (E5). The gatekeeper-egress translates the factory model API into each provider's native format and meters every call the same way, whatever the provider.
* **M4 – Native access is an exception.** An agent that genuinely needs a provider-specific feature may declare that need and use the provider's native format through the gatekeeper-egress, still metered and policy-gated. Such an agent is provider-dependent, and its admission shows that.

### 6.10 Ledger Integrity
* **LG1 – Single writer.** Exactly one control-plane process writes a ledger at any time, enforced twice: the control plane holds an exclusive, renewed lease on the ledger before it starts (a second instance refuses to start and says why; a lease left by a crashed instance expires after a short timeout), and every landing zone deploys the control plane stop-then-start, never side by side. A control-plane deploy therefore has a brief gap (accepted 2026-09-28); running concurrent control planes would require moving the ledger off a shared file.
* **LG2 – Never edited.** A ledger that fails verification is archived unchanged as evidence, never repaired in place. Recording continues in a new segment whose first entry chains to the last verified checkpoint and records the recovery (why, and what was archived).

### 6.11 Keymaster: Credentials That Act for a Person or an App
* **K1 – The Keymaster owns them.** OAuth grants, refresh tokens, and app client secrets are held only by the Keymaster. Agents declare the *connections* they need (system plus scopes) in their cartridge; admission shows them; the agent never holds the credential. How each provider's sign-in and tokens work comes from its approved system definition (E10), never from agent or platform code.
* **K2 – Consent happens in the factory.** A person connects an agent to a provider through the factory. The provider redirects back to the factory; the Keymaster stores the grant scoped to agent × provider × scopes; the ledger records who granted what, and when.
* **K3 – The Keymaster refreshes; the gatekeeper-egress injects.** The agent calls a gatekeeper-egress route; the gatekeeper-egress obtains a current access token from the Keymaster (which refreshes and persists rotated tokens) and injects it.
* **K4 – Broken consent is reported, never worked around.** A revoked or expired grant is marked "needs re-consent". A provider that issues no refresh token (LinkedIn) gives a grant that ends when its access token does; the Keymaster reports when it ends, so the person can reconnect before then, and marks it at expiry; the agent is told so and directs the person to the factory's reconnect link. Agents never ask a person to run scripts or handle credentials.
* **K5 – The Keymaster facilitates every credential an agent needs.**
  1. *Declared at registration.* An agent declares every credential it uses, each with its kind (static secret or OAuth connection), the source system it comes from, and scopes where they apply. This includes the credentials the Gatekeeper uses on the agent's behalf: each trigger's `secretRef` and each per-agent route credential (`{agent}_X`). An agent declares a logical name; the Keymaster creates and names every entry itself (`agents/<agent>/<system>/<name>` for an agent's own credential, `shared/<system>/<name>` for a shared one), so an agent cannot name another agent's credential or a platform key. From the declaration the Keymaster knows where the credential is stored and how it is obtained (E8).
  2. *Outstanding credentials are known.* The Keymaster compares each registered agent's declarations with what is present and valid in the secret manager. A credential that is missing, lacks declared scopes, or needs re-consent is outstanding for that agent.
  3. *One flow, every surface.* The Keymaster API is the flow; the dashboard and the Garrison interface are clients of it and add no logic of their own. In each, the owner picks an agent and sees its outstanding credentials, each with instructions for creating it and a way to supply it: OAuth through a consent URL whose callback lets the Keymaster store the grant; static secrets (for example GitHub PATs, Discord bot tokens, Slack keys, API keys) through a write-only input the Keymaster writes to the secret manager. A supplied value is never displayed, echoed, logged, or ledgered; the ledger records only that it was set or rotated, by whom, and when.
  4. *Instructions come from a reviewed catalog.* The Keymaster keeps a catalog of instructions per source system, maintained by operations. Entries may be AI-drafted; a person reviews and approves each one. Until then an entry is shown to owners clearly labelled "Pending human review", never presented as approved.
  5. *Access is policy, not possession.* A credential may serve several agents (for example one workspace integration). Because agents never hold it (S1), which agents may use it is decided by factory policy (routes and agent state), not by who has the value: revoking one agent's access (pause, decommission, destroy, or removing the route) never requires rotating the credential, and rotating a credential never involves an agent.
  6. *Addressed to the agent owner* (an owner and an admin may supply an agent's credentials; owner rights are the Bouncer's, GAP-088). No one is asked to run a script or to put a secret in a chat, terminal, or repository.


### 6.12 Operator Access
* **A1 – One front door.** The factory's public endpoints accept connections only from its identity-aware proxy (Cloudflare Access on AWS); the load balancer refuses all other sources. Machine callers (deploy checks, webhooks) use the same door with a service credential. No landing zone opens platform ingress to `0.0.0.0/0`.
* **A2 – Identity is verified, not asserted.** The control plane grants a role only from a credential it verifies cryptographically: a factory token, a run token, or the proxy's signed assertion checked against its issuer and audience. An identity header alone grants nothing, and no proxy (the console included) adds a privileged credential on a caller's behalf.


### 6.13 Recoverability
* **R1 – The factory's own state is recoverable.** Everything only the factory holds (agent registrations and deployment configuration, policies, approvals, schedules, runs) is backed up automatically and can be restored to an earlier point in time. The ledger (§6.10), credentials (§6.11) and agent memory (§6.6) keep their own protection. Restoring is a documented operation, rehearsed at least once. The deployment configuration store (SK3) is versioned with point-in-time restore and can be exported.


### 6.14 Skills and Deployment Configuration
* **SK1 – Skills are registered, admitted and approved per version.** Any authenticated user may register a skill by naming a repository, a path and a commit (registering a private skill needs an admin or an owner of the named agent): one repo per skill, a skills monorepo, or a folder inside an agent's repo. A skill is its source: a different repository or path is a different skill, never a new version of an existing one. A skill is **public** (any agent may be given it, SK3) or **private** (it names one owner agent in its registration and only that agent may adopt it). Visibility and owner are set at registration and never change; to change either, register a new skill. Admission pins the commit, runs the skill's tests and checks the design rules (no secrets, no direct hosts, no provider SDKs). Every version, public or private, not just the first, is approved by a factory admin before any agent can adopt it, and only after the factory has built it, run its tests and checked its code against these rules, as a pull request merges only when its required checks pass. Approving a version makes it *available*; it adopts nothing for a public skill (SK3). Refusals, approvals and retirements are ledgered.
* **SK2 – A skill declares what it needs and what it does, and holds nothing.** A skill's manifest declares the routes, connections (with scopes) and credentials it requires, and its **actions**: each action names the system route, method and path it takes (E11) and says `hold: true` or `hold: false`. `hold: true` means the action is human-in-the-loop: the gatekeeper-egress forwards nothing until a person has approved that exact request, and the approval is written to the ledger before the action is allowed (E9). `hold: false` means autonomous. A manifest with an action that declares no `hold` is refused (no default, E9). Today the gatekeeper-egress cannot tell actions on one route apart (GAP-070), so a declared `hold` is recorded and shown to the approver but is enforced only once grants carry actions (E11, TSK-068). A skill never holds a secret (S1) and never grants access; what it can do inside an agent is bounded by that agent's policy (E7). The skill's declared holds are the author's declaration (E9); an admin reviews them at version approval and may change them, and may change any grant's hold at any time. Enforcement is per agent; a skill that must be isolated from its host's other grants runs as a separate service with its own identity.
* **SK3 – An agent's deployment configuration lives in the factory, and adoption is part of it.** Each agent has one versioned configuration record: its pinned source (repo and commit), the skills it has adopted (each pinned to an approved version), and its policy. Every change creates a new version recording who, when and why, and is ledgered with a content hash. Adopting, upgrading, holding, rolling back or removing a skill is a configuration change made in the factory, never a pull request or other change in `agent-factory`, `submind-aws` or any agent or skill repository. Adopting a **public** skill is requested by the agent's owner (or, from conversation, by the agent, SK4) for a named agent and an approved version; the request always needs an admin's decision (the admin who sets policy, E7) and takes effect only when approved. Approving an adoption does not grant access: the agent's policy still names the routes (SK2). A **private** skill is adopted for its owner agent automatically, in the same configuration change as the approval of each of its versions, unless that agent's adoption was revoked (SK6); no one may adopt it for any other agent, and the factory refuses the attempt. The admin who approves the version is recorded as the approver of the adoption, and the adoption has its own ledger row, marked automatic. Approving a version (SK1) and approving an agent's adoption of it are separate decisions with separate ledger rows; neither implies the other for a public skill. A pending adoption request is not part of the configuration: it creates no configuration version and no build, and only an approved adoption (or a removal) writes a new version and changes the build hash (SK4).
* **SK4 – What runs is exactly the configuration; a change redeploys.** Applying a configuration builds one image from the agent's source at its commit plus each adopted skill at its pinned commit, tagged by the source commit and a hash of the pinned skills (never the configuration hash, so a policy or owner change never rebuilds; an agent with no skills keeps the tag `<agent>-<commit>` it always had), admitted and deployed (L3, L4). The Property Manager builds the image, places each skill in it with a manifest of the adopted skills, and names that manifest in the agent's environment at launch; the factory delivers the files and the manifest, and how an agent invokes a skill is the cartridge's (the Donna Test). No code is ever loaded at runtime and nothing needs a pull request: a new configuration version triggers the build and redeploys the agent, which restarts to activate it. An agent's request in conversation to adopt a skill creates an adoption request (SK3) that a person approves. An agent whose configuration did not change is not rebuilt or restarted.
* **SK5 – New skill versions are offered, never forced.** When a newer approved version of an adopted public skill exists, the factory shows "skill update available" to the agent's owner (as L5 does for agents). The owner decides when to upgrade. A private skill's newly approved version is adopted by its owner agent automatically (SK3) unless the owner agent's adoption was revoked (SK6); that is the one exception.
* **SK6 – Skills are retired and adoptions revoked separately.** An admin may **retire** a skill: no new version can be approved or adopted, and every approved version stays recorded. Retiring a skill, or revoking one approved version, that any agent's deployed configuration uses is refused until those agents are redeployed without it; an admin override retires or revokes it at once, pauses every agent using it, removes it from each configuration and redeploys each without it (SK4), and the agent is resumed when that completes. An agent's owner or an admin may **revoke one agent's adoption** of a skill: that writes a new configuration version without it (SK3) and redeploys the agent (SK4). Revoking an adoption of a private skill by its owner agent leaves the skill registered and unadopted, and stops its later versions from being adopted automatically; the owner agent adopts it again only by a new adoption request that an admin approves (SK3), because a revocation is a deliberate decision that a new version must not silently undo. Retirements, revocations and the pauses they cause are ledgered.
* **SK7 – The factory shows what is registered and who uses it.** A factory API returns every registered skill with its visibility (public or private), its owner agent (private only), its versions with status, check results and declared actions and holds, and the agents that have adopted each version (and any adoption requests awaiting approval). The same API answers the reverse: the skills one agent has adopted. The console is a client of that API only, and from it an authorized person registers a skill, approves or rejects a version, retires a skill, requests, approves and revokes an agent's adoption, and sees "update available" (SK5). Every one of those actions asks the Bouncer for a named privilege (§6.15). A private skill is visible only to admins and to the owners of its owner agent.
* **SK8 – Existing in-repo skills are private skills until split.** A skill that an agent carries and declares inline in its own `cartridge.yaml` (today's agents) is treated as a private skill of that agent. It is registered, approved per version and adopted under SK1 to SK4 like any other, and the inline declaration is retired when it is. A skill that more than one agent should use is split out as a public skill by registering it as a new skill (SK1); that is later, separate work. Until an agent's skill is registered, the agent's own repository remains its only source for it (E8) and the registry shows it as unregistered.

### 6.15 Factory Service Architecture (The Cast of Characters) & First-Class Contracts
* **SV1 – Intentional Services.** Every workspace package belongs to one of the 10 recognized services (Gatekeeper, Keymaster, Executive, Property Manager, Auditor, Accountant, Bouncer, Timekeeper, Registrar, Inspector), to declared platform tooling (`console`, `conformance`, `bench`), or to a declared reservation for a named service that has no implementation yet (none today). A service's `hostedIn` entries are workspace packages that contain its implementation source; a directory that merely exists is not a host. Introducing a new service requires explicit architectural intent, naming the new cast member, and user confirmation before implementation. Incidental or stealth service creation is prohibited.
* **SV2 – First-Class Cross-Service Contracts.** Services communicate across boundaries strictly through explicit, typed contracts wired directly into real call sites. Direct backdoor access to another service's private persistence, tables, files, or internal modules without a formal contract is forbidden.

#### The 10 Factory Services (The Cast):
1. **Gatekeeper (Perimeter Security):** Owns inbound presence, wakes, webhooks, front-door authentication verification (A1, A2), and zero-trust outbound proxying (E1, E6) with credential injection (S1), without deciding what a caller may do or answering for another member. It authenticates and never authorizes: `packages/auth` verifies a credential and yields a principal with its roles (A2) and stays its own package, hosted by the Gatekeeper as `packages/telemetry` is by the Inspector; what a principal may do is the Bouncer's. `packages/gatekeeper` holds the egress (`src/egress`: `createGatekeeperEgress`, the run-token check, route and host allowlist, credential injection, metering, the tool allowlist and the asks to the Bouncer; its own process and image), the ingress (`src/ingress`: `createGatekeeperIngress`, Discord presence and wakes; its own process and image, built from `ingress.Dockerfile`), the policy store and its validation (`PolicyStore`, `validatePolicy`, `AgentPolicy`, `GLOBAL_POLICY_ID`, `isReservedPolicyId`) and one typed contract, `verifyRun`: whether a bearer token speaks for a live run of the agent it names. Ingress manages the interactive Discord turn lifecycle (wakes, typing heartbeat, in-channel live throttled progress updates, and final token/tool execution summaries without cartridge involvement, GAP-186, TSK-252). It answers not a run token (the caller may try a person), refused (an unknown run, another agent's run, the wrong run or an ended run; never falls back to a person) or ok with the claims and the run; the token check, the run lookup and what "ended" means are passed in as plain values, so the Gatekeeper never imports the Property Manager. Its entry points are the root, the listed subpath `./policy` and the listed subpath `./run-token`, nothing deeper (D1), so the control plane loads the policy and run-token code and not the egress server. The Gatekeeper asks the Accountant (`checkStanding`), the Bouncer (the hold rules), the Executive (`complete`, `checkModel`) and the Keymaster (the sign-in link rule, `stripSignInLinksFromJson`) through their contracts and enforces the answers; it imports no caller (`control-plane`, `console`) and no member above it (`property-manager`, `timekeeper`), and no package below it imports it back. What stays outside: the HTTP handlers that call the Gatekeeper (`authenticateRun`, the spend, progress, schedules, identity-links and policy routes), in `control-plane`, because that is where the Bouncer's privileges and the Auditor's ledger rows meet a request (GAP-133); and the Keymaster's "is the run live" lookup (`getRun`), which is a second copy of what `verifyRun` answers and is left open (GAP-137 part 2). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/gatekeeper.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, on a run token verified by hand outside the Gatekeeper (no exception: the Keymaster's own check was removed, GAP-137 part 1), on a back-dependency and on an entry point beyond the three.
2. **Keymaster (Credential Facilitation):** Owns OAuth connections, consent flows, token refreshes (K1–K4), write-only static secret onboarding (K5), and vault mapping.
3. **Executive (Model Service / Inference Provider):** Serves the Factory's models without answering a request or holding a credential. `packages/executive` holds the model catalog (`parseModelCatalog`, `findModel`; the offering is operations data, M3), the OpenAI-compatible request rules (M1: `parseChatRequest`), the provider adapters and their translation (`bedrockConverse`, `toConverse`, `fromConverse`, `defaultModelAdapters`, `ModelUpstreamError`), AWS request signing (`signV4`, `awsCredentialsFromEnv`), token counting (E5: `usageFromJson`, `SseMeter`, `Usage`) and three typed contracts: `checkModel` and `offeredModels` (whether an agent may use a model, M2 and E7: the policy's allow-list, the factory default when a policy names none, the training exemption and a run's pin; plain values in, so the Executive does not import the control plane's `Policy`) and `complete` (serve one call: validate the request, find the provider's adapter, call it; it returns the result, a refusal made before any provider was called, or the upstream failure, and never throws). The Executive decides and reports; it reports each call's usage and the Accountant prices it and decides standing. What stays outside: the `handleModels` and `handleLlm` handlers in `gatekeeper-egress.ts`, which are the Gatekeeper's, because that is where the run token, the throttle, the price check, the Auditor's ledger rows, the Inspector's metrics and traces and the OpenAI-format response body meet a request (GAP-130). There is no default-model constant: the default is configuration (M2, GAP-181). The Executive depends on the Accountant for the `Price` and `TokenUsage` types only, and never on the egress, the control plane or the console. A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/executive.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules or a back-dependency.
4. **Property Manager (Compute & State Lifecycle, Turn Broker):** Manages serverless min=0 compute, wake-from-zero, warm-down windows, operational pause/kill-switches, turn coordination via `/mailbox` long-polling, container runtime state hydration ("The Safe", §6.6), and two-stage retirement (§6.4), without answering a request or holding a credential. `packages/property-manager` holds the run lifecycle (`createRun`, `finishRun`, `blockRun`, `unblockRun`, `activeRun`, the idle timeout, `deliverToMailbox`, `resetAgent`, `applyKillSwitch`, `haltUnhealthy`, `checkHealth`, `routeEvents`, `reconcileRuns`, `SYSTEM`), the run store and run tokens (`MemoryRunStore`, `FileRunStore`, `RunTokens`), the SQS queue pollers (`pollQueueOnce`, `startQueuePollers`), signed callback delivery (`deliverCallback`, `checkCallbackUrl`), the runtime launchers (`Runtime`, `memoryRuntime`, `noopRuntime`, `dockerRuntime`, `ecsRuntime`), the build-side types and helpers the adapters share (`SourceRef`, `BuildSkill`, `DeployProvider`, `AdmissionRefusedError`, `imageTagFor`, `skillsHash`), and the AWS and GCP image build, IAM and task or job registration (`./aws/*`, `./gcp/*`, loaded lazily so a deployment pulls in only its own cloud's SDKs). `packages/hydrate` holds the in-container runtime shim (`shim.ts`, `pullMind`, `pushMind`, `gatekeeperEgressEnv`) that executes inside the agent container as PID 1 to hydrate the notebook into `$MEMORY_DIR` on wake, sync state on sleep, heartbeat to the control plane, bridge decoupled inputs and outputs, and supervise the worker process. The functions take `PropertyManagerState`, the part of the control plane's `FactoryState` they use, and the queue pollers and the Timekeeper's `fire` take `createRun` as a callback, so the package never imports the control plane. The Property Manager asks the Accountant for standing (`checkStanding`) before it wakes an agent, and the Registrar for the agent record and the build (`admit` takes the Property Manager's build as a callback). What stays outside: the HTTP handlers and `FactoryState` in `control-plane` (GAP-133), and the Registrar's skill-check runner (`control-plane/src/aws/skill-checker.ts`, the half of `aws/codebuild.ts` that was the Registrar's). The lifecycle names two cartridges by id for crash and budget-alert routing (GAP-133). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/property-manager.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, a second copy of the cloud SDK use, or a back-dependency.
5. **Auditor (Immutable Ledger):** Owns the cryptographic append-only WORM audit trail of runs, spend, credential actions, and perimeter decisions (LG1, LG2) with automatic secret and prompt redaction.
6. **Accountant (Spend Governance & FinOps):** Owns the budget rule in `packages/budget` and the factory cloud spend, compute inventory reporting, and factory budget governance. `checkStanding` is the typed contract for whether an agent is inside its limits (unacknowledged spend included when the caller has it). `packages/budget` holds the budget rule, spend accounting, pricing, the standing contract, cloud cost aggregation, compute inventory attribution, and factory-wide budget tracking. The Accountant holds dedicated read-only cloud credentials (`factory-prod-accountant` role) for Cost Explorer and ECS cluster inventory; agents never hold direct AWS routes or credentials. The control plane exposes the Accountant's reports via `GET /api/v1/spend/report`, `GET /api/v1/spend/compute`, `GET /api/v1/spend/budgets`, and `PUT /api/v1/spend/budgets`. Model spend and cloud infrastructure spend are covered by the single `factory-spend` tool grant. Budgets are evaluated on gross usage (before credits). There is no default budget (Dale, chat, 2026-10-10): neither the factory's monthly budget nor any agent's is created by default or seeded by code; an admin allocates each explicitly through the API (E7), and until one is set nothing is enforced or alerted on for it. Agent compute is reporting-only. The gatekeeper-egress asks before every model call, the Property Manager asks before every wake (`createRun`), the control plane asks before a message goes into a live run, and it also asks on ledger ingest and on a policy update. Those callers enforce the answer (`budget.alert`, `blockRun`, HTTP 402); the Gatekeeper's ingress relays a refused wake or message to the person. A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/accountant.test.ts`) fails if the gatekeeper-egress or control-plane source compares spend with a limit itself.
7. **Bouncer (Authorization & Approvals):** `packages/bouncer` decides who may do what and holds what must wait for a person. Authorization is role-based in every case: a user maps to roles and a role maps to privileges, never a user to a privilege, and the table is built into the code (making it factory data an admin edits is a later enhancement). Every control-plane route asks the Bouncer for one named privilege (`authorize`, `requirePrivilege`); the roles are the six `auth` emits plus three derived from data, never from a credential: `agent-owner` (a person on the agent's configuration record, SK3), `agent-member` (a person holding an agent role on the agent, E12: it may start runs and converse on that agent and do nothing else) and `requester` (the person whose message started a run, set only by the ingress). An owner may wake, pause and resume the agent, view and export its configuration, supply its credentials and start its connections, and decide its held actions when the run has no requesting user. For an agent's action the agent is the actor and the requesting user is the reviewer and approver; the global `admin` and `approver` roles also decide any agent's. The Bouncer also owns the identity links: the list an admin keeps of which Discord, Slack, Cloudflare or Entra identities are one person (`IdentityLinkStore` in `packages/bouncer`, the `/api/v1/identity-links` handler in `control-plane`), because a link exists only to decide what that person may do (Dale, chat, 2026-10-12, GAP-136); the Gatekeeper verifies a credential and the ingress asks the Bouncer who the sender is. The Bouncer also owns the approval record and the E9 hold rules (the reviewed copy, its hash and size limit, which requests are held and which tool calls need approval); the gatekeeper-egress asks it and enforces the answer, and the control plane acts on a decision (`blockRun`, `deliverDecision`). Conformance fails on a role check outside `packages/bouncer` and `packages/auth`, on a privilege that is not in the contract, and on a route the matrix test does not list (`packages/conformance/src/authorization.test.ts`).
8. **Timekeeper (Scheduling & Timers):** Decides when an agent is due, without persistent in-container daemon processes. `packages/timekeeper` holds the cron rule (`cronMatches`, `agentsDueForCron`), time zones and cron validation (`getZonedTimeParts`, `isTimeZone`, `cronIssue`, `DEFAULT_TIMEZONE`), the schedule record and its store (`ScheduledAction`, `ScheduleStore` with `checkDue`, persisted as `schedules.json` at `FACTORY_SCHEDULES_PATH`, else `DATA_DIR/schedules.json`), and the contract and the clock: `createTimekeeper({ agents, schedules, fire })` asks every minute which cartridge crons and which schedules are due and calls `fire` with a `FireRequest` (`{ kind: 'cartridge', agentId }` or `{ kind: 'schedule', schedule }`). It never starts a run and never imports the control plane; the composition root (`control-plane/src/index.ts`) is the only caller, and its `fire` is the Property Manager's side (`createRun`, and the rule that a cartridge cron is skipped while the agent is already running). What stays outside: the `/api/v1/schedules` handler stays in `control-plane/src/schedules.ts` and owns the run-token scoping (the Gatekeeper's, GAP-089), the Bouncer privilege calls and the ledger rows (the Auditor's), because those meet a request there. One-shot wakeup timers are not built (GAP-096). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/timekeeper.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of the cron rule, a second scheduler loop or a back-dependency.
9. **Registrar (Admissions & Catalog):** Decides what is admitted and what is recorded about it, without building an image or serving a request. `packages/registrar` holds the agent record and the built-in agents (`AgentRecord`, `loadCatalog`, `loadDynamicRegistry`, `mergeAgents`), the registry store (`AgentRegistry`: `save`, `update` and `remove` over `<registryDir>/<id>.json`), source pinning (`FULL_SHA`, `checkRepoUrl`, `gitSkillSource`), the factory's checks on a skill's code (`SkillChecker`, `localSkillChecker`, `SKILL_CHECK_SCRIPT`), the skill registry (`SkillRegistry`, `summarizeSkill`, `compareSemver`, `checkSkillPath`, `fetchManifest`), the deployment configuration store (`VersionedConfigStore` over a directory or S3 backend, `configHash`, `archiveStamp`) the system definition store (E10: `SystemsStore`, written through to `<dir>/<id>/<version>.json`, with a ledger row for each proposal, decision and import) and the admission contract (`pinSource`, `beginAdmission`, `admit`, `admissionOf`, `stateAfterRefusal`). `admit` takes a source and the Property Manager's build callback, changes no record and cannot deploy, so a deploy today and an admin admitting a newly seen commit later (L5) call the same function. What stays outside: the HTTP handlers (`handleConfig`, `handleSkills`, `handleSystems` and the registry and deploy routes in `app.ts`), because that is where the Bouncer's privileges, the Auditor's ledger rows and the Gatekeeper's run-token rule meet a request (GAP-099); the image build, identity and compute registration (the Property Manager's); `skillCheckerFromEnv`, which picks the deployment's skill checker and loads the Property Manager's CodeBuild one (`control-plane/src/skills.ts`); registration, which still pins inline (GAP-101); orphan pruning, which reads the registry and the Gatekeeper's policy store together (GAP-100); and the two methods that turn a system's OAuth description into a Keymaster connection provider, which are on a subclass of the store in `control-plane/src/systems.ts` so that the Registrar does not depend on the Keymaster (D2, Dale's option A). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/registrar.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, an admission record built by hand, a write to the registry directory outside the store, or a back-dependency. The agent version flow (a new commit alerts, an admin admits it, the admin or the owner deploys it; L5, GAP-037) is not part of the extraction: `docs/registrar-roadmap.md`.
10. **Inspector (Observability, Telemetry & Triage):** Watches the factory and says what it sees, without deciding anything or answering a request. `packages/inspector` holds the contract (`createInspector`: `publish`, `publishRun`, `subscribe`, `tapLedger`, `attachBus`, `reportProgress`, `readProgress`, `stop`), the event hub and the bus sinks (`EventHub`, `isBusWorthy`, `fileSink`, `eventBridgeSink`, `busSinkFromEnv`, `attachBus`), the per-run progress ring and its sanitizer (`RunProgress`, `sanitizeProgress`), the egress's progress emitter (`ProgressEmitter`, `ProgressCall`, `safeIdent`), prompt traces (`writeTrace`, `pruneTraces`, `traceConfigFromEnv`), the run metrics instruments (`factoryMetrics`) and the triage rule (`incidentsFromRuns`). `packages/telemetry` (`initTelemetry`) stays a separate package the Inspector hosts (D-A). What stays outside: the HTTP handlers (`control-plane/src/progress-routes.ts`, `stream.ts`, and the `/api/v1/metrics` and `/api/v1/triage` routes in `app.ts`), because that is where the Bouncer's privileges and the Gatekeeper's run-token rule meet a request (GAP-104); the `/api/v1/metrics` aggregate, which reads five members' data (GAP-106); the egress's own request, latency, token and cost instruments (GAP-109). The empty `packages/triage` placeholder (a README for an alert router that was never built) was removed (D-B); the router is GAP-107. The Inspector does no authorization and never reads a run: the caller passes in whether a reported run is live. `tapLedger` still wraps the Auditor's store (GAP-105). The role text used to say it routes crash diagnostics to alerting; what exists is the derived incident list and the bus sink (GAP-107). A conformance test (`packages/conformance/src/inspector.test.ts`) fails on a second copy of these rules, a second Inspector, or a back-dependency.

#### Architectural Boundaries:
* **The Cartridge vs. Factory Boundary ("The Donna Test"):** Cartridges (the employees) own business logic, prompts, persona, and internal cognitive reasoning loops. The Factory provides the utilities (compute, memory safe, model service, perimeter).
* **The Console vs. Factory Boundary (Headless Principle):** The web Console and Garrison are headless client overlays (§6.5). They own zero service logic, zero secrets, and zero persistence.
* **The Four Deployable Units (Deploy & Lifecycle Boundary, Dale, chat 2026-10-19, GAP-187):** While the 10 Cast members are partitioned into dedicated packages with strict compile-time contract boundaries, the runtime deployment topology comprises exactly four deployable factory units:
  1. `console`: The headless web management overlay (`packages/console/Dockerfile`). Independently versioned via SemVer (`0.2.0`), deployable at high velocity without restarting or cycling the factory kernel or active agent runs.
  2. `control-plane`: The Factory Kernel (`packages/control-plane/Dockerfile`), composing the 8 core Cast members (Property Manager, Accountant, Bouncer, Executive, Registrar, Inspector, Keymaster, Auditor, Timekeeper) into a single transactional process with atomic in-memory hot paths and the single-writer WORM ledger (LG1).
  3. `gatekeeper-egress`: The egress perimeter proxy (`packages/gatekeeper/Dockerfile`) in the agent VPC/subnet, enforcing zero-trust egress, run-token verification, credential injection, and model token metering.
  4. `gatekeeper-ingress`: The ingress edge service (`packages/gatekeeper/ingress.Dockerfile`) terminating external webhooks and Discord gateway WebSockets.
  *(Plus the independent agent cartridges, which wake and sleep on demand).*

Each member that shares a package is extracted into its own package behind a typed contract, one member at a time (the Treasurer flow, GAP-083, is the worked example). A change is then inside one member or on one contract.

**Rules for every extraction.**
- Two lanes. Anything that edits `packages/control-plane/src/app.ts` runs in series with every other task that edits it. Work confined to another package may run in parallel.
- Moves change no behavior; new behavior is its own later task. Every task is green on its own.
- One plan per member, written when its turn comes, in the Treasurer shape: create the package and move the rule; move the data; define the contract; wire the callers; add a guard against a second copy; close out §6.15. Decisions that are Dale's are written as "Confirmed by Dale" before any task is handed to an executor; the plan is checked against the code by someone other than its author before handoff.
- Do not run an executor in a clone that another session is using; give each its own clone or worktree.

**Bouncer decisions already made by Dale (chat, 2026-10-03).** Authentication stays with the Gatekeeper and authorization belongs to the Bouncer. There must be an `agent-owner` role and every agent can identify its owner or owners. Access is role-based in all cases: users map to roles and roles map to privileges, with no direct user-to-privilege mapping. The actor and the approver are separate parties; for an agent's action the agent is the actor and the requesting user is the reviewer and approver, and for each other privileged action type the plan states who the parties are. Decided for the Bouncer plan (Dale, chat, 2026-10-03): the role-to-privilege table is built into the code now, and making it factory data that an admin edits is a later enhancement; an agent's owners are stored on its configuration record (SK3); putting the control plane behind the Gatekeeper is out of the Bouncer flow; the Gatekeeper's ingress passes the Discord author id with a wake and an admin links Discord ids to people; a run with no requesting user is decided by any owner of the agent; the `agent-owner` role holds the rights to decide held actions, view and export the agent's configuration, supply its credentials, and wake, pause and resume it; and the global `admin` and `approver` roles keep deciding any agent's held action. The plan and its remaining open items are in the Bouncer flow work stream (§5).

### 6.16 Action Provenance & Anti-Hallucination Invariant (Multi-Surface Egress & Turn Boundary Verification)
* **AP1 – Dual Defense Boundary:**
  1. *Primary Source / Egress Layer (`gatekeeper-egress` / Executive):* Model inference calls support native tool schemas (`tools` and `tool_choice`) across all supported model providers (AWS Bedrock Converse, OpenAI, xAI, Anthropic Claude, and Google Vertex Gemini). When `tools` are declared in the completion request, raw textual mimicry of tool calls (e.g. ````name: {...}````) without structured `tool_calls` is rejected immediately with HTTP 422 `malformed_tool_syntax`, and the agent retries normally. The factory never forces a tool choice in response: newer models reject forced tool choice (GAP-175), and forcing one turned a rejected reply into a dead end (2026-10-18).
  2. *Turn Boundary Layer (`control-plane` / Property Manager):* Prior to committing a turn response or delivering it to any outbound surface (Discord, Slack, Web Console, Gaming Chat), the platform verifies action provenance. Any claimed execution of state-altering external side-effects (e.g. social posts, git commits/PRs, webhook pushes) MUST be backed by attested upstream egress receipts in the current run's immutable ledger.
* **AP2 – Unverified Claim Mitigation:**
  If an agent generates text asserting that an external state-altering action succeeded, but no matching egress receipt or released held request exists in the run's audit trail, the platform replaces the unverified assertion with a standardized safety banner:
  `⚠️ Action unverified: Agent claimed to complete an action, but no upstream receipt was found in the audit trail. Nothing was executed.`
  and records an `UNVERIFIED_ACTION_CLAIM` security event to the Auditor's immutable ledger.
* **AP3 – Answers without tools are footnoted (the user's decision, 2026-10-18):**
  1. *Warn up front.* Whenever an agent's model request includes tools, the gatekeeper-egress adds a standing note telling the model that replies given without calling a tool are delivered with the footnote `[no tools called]`.
  2. *Footnote.* A turn's final model answer, given with tools offered and nothing consulted for it, is recorded as `UNGROUNDED_REPLY` and reaches the user ending in `[no tools called]`. Consulted means either tool results in the turn, or a read the run made, as the gatekeeper-egress itself recorded, before the model request arrived, within the last 15 minutes, and not already used by an earlier answer: a `GET`/`HEAD` the upstream answered 2xx on a data route (http or Keymaster connection; never the Discord route), or an MCP `tools/call` whose result is not an error. A write consults nothing, and a model call without tools uses nothing up (the user's decision, 2026-10-19: an agent's own code may fetch the data and give it to the model as text). Not covered: tool messages the agent writes itself are trusted; reads are per run, not per person; reads through the forward proxy or a CONNECT tunnel, searches sent as `POST`, and MCP tool results larger than 256 KiB are not counted (they can only add a footnote); a `GET` that changes something upstream counts as a read; reads before a model call that failed are not used up, so for up to 15 minutes they can ground the next answer. Age and use are judged when the model request arrives.
  3. *Warn after, per agent.* With `grounding: warn-once` in an agent's policy (set by its owner), the gatekeeper-egress gives the model one more chance with the notice before returning a no-tool answer. Both calls are metered and recorded (`GROUNDING_RETRY`).
  4. *Where footnotes apply.* On every reply path the factory controls: the Discord route, the console reply once it exists, and the run result.

### 6.17 Command-Line Client and Factory Manager (the user's decision, 2026-10-21)
A terminal client for macOS (`factory`, `packages/cli`, declared platform tooling like `console`, SV1) and an optional Factory Manager cartridge. The CLI is a client of the existing API, WebSocket and event interfaces; it adds no interface (KPF) and no factory logic of its own, as the console.
* **CL1 – Pointed at an instance, signed in as a person (A1, A2).** The CLI opens with no instance chosen and nobody signed in: it shows a splash screen with the state (instance, how it signs in, who is signed in) and offers `/config` and `/login`. `/config` sets the factory address and terminal preferences; `/login` signs in, signs out, or signs in as a different user, by the method the deployment uses (CL9). `factory login <url>` remains as a shortcut for the same. What the CLI sends is a credential it obtained by signing the person in (a Cloudflare Access assertion or an OIDC token), kept in the macOS Keychain and refreshed before it expires; the factory verifies it (A2). The CLI never holds `FACTORY_TOKEN`, never sends an identity header of its own, and a factory that does not verify the credential refuses it. Nothing in the configuration is a credential.
* **CL2 – The manager is house staff, not a service (SV1, L2, L5, Donna Test).** The Factory Manager is a cartridge in `agents/staff`, admitted by an operator like the Personal Trainer. It answers and decides as an agent does; no kernel package gains reasoning, memory or conversation behavior. The ten platform services stay non-generative.
* **CL3 – The person's roles decide what happens (E12).** The manager has no standing power over the factory. Every read and every change runs with the signed-in person's own credential and roles, so a viewer sees what a viewer sees and only an admin sets a budget. A refusal (401, 403) reaches the person as the factory worded it.
* **CL4 – The manager proposes, the CLI executes.** The manager returns tool requests (list agents, read spend, set a budget, and so on); the CLI runs each against the factory API as the person, shows the result to the manager, and asks the person to confirm before any call that changes state. Calls that change state are ledgered under the person's identity as in the console. The CLI offers the manager only the tools that the roles in `GET /api/v1/whoami` allow, judged by the Bouncer's own `authorize`; the factory still decides every call.
* **CL5 – Connect to an agent.** The person can ask the manager to connect them to an agent. The CLI then converses with that agent directly through `POST /api/v1/agents/:id/conversation` (privilege `agents.converse`, budget and Bouncer checks unchanged) and reads its answers from `GET /api/v1/agents/:id/replies`, the console's existing reply path (AM2); it needs no new interface. The CLI returns to the manager on request. The agent sees the person as the caller (AM2); the manager is not in the path. The manager itself is reached the same way.
* **CL6 – Nothing sensitive in the terminal (K5, S1).** The CLI and manager never print, store or accept a secret value, an unmasked credential or a provider key; credential supply stays on the Keymaster's write-only flow.
* **CL7 – Answers are grounded (AP1 to AP3).** Figures and states the manager reports come from tool results in that turn; an answer given without a tool call carries the factory's footnote.
* **CL8 – Setup is part of the conversation, and preferences are the person's own.** `/config` can be run at any time, signed in or not, to view or change the factory address and terminal preferences (theme: `auto`, `dark`, `light` or `mono`; plain output when `NO_COLOR` is set or the output is not a terminal). Changing the address signs the person out of the old one (a sign-in belongs to one instance). `/login` never shows or stores a secret (CL6). Preferences live in the CLI's own config file, readable by its owner only, and are never sent to the factory or the manager.
* **CL9 – A deployment says how its people sign in; the CLI follows (A1, A2, K5, S1).** The CLI supports two sign-in methods and learns which one applies. (1) *Cloudflare Access* (the identity-aware proxy): detected when an unauthenticated request is redirected to the proxy's sign-in, or named by the ops repository's installer. (2) *OIDC* (any provider the factory verifies with `FACTORY_OIDC_ISSUER` and `FACTORY_OIDC_AUDIENCE`: Entra, Google, Cognito, Okta): the factory publishes a small public document at `GET /.well-known/factory-cli` with the issuer, the public client id the CLI signs in with, the scopes, which token to present (`id_token` or `access_token`) and, when the provider needs one, a fixed loopback port. The document is built from deployment configuration (`FACTORY_OIDC_CLI_CLIENT_ID`, `FACTORY_OIDC_CLI_SCOPES`, `FACTORY_OIDC_CLI_TOKEN_USE`, `FACTORY_OIDC_CLI_REDIRECT_PORT`), holds no secret, and is served only when `FACTORY_OIDC_CLI_CLIENT_ID` is set. The OIDC sign-in is authorization code with PKCE (S256) and a state and nonce, using a loopback redirect on 127.0.0.1 only; the CLI has no client secret. The token is sent as `Authorization: Bearer`, which the factory verifies like any other (A2). Signing in as a different user asks the provider to re-authenticate (`prompt=login`). Where a proxy fronts the factory (A1) nothing is reachable before sign-in, so the discovery route is for deployments without one; a proxy is recognised by its redirect.
* **CL10 – The CLI is installed from the ops repository, which seeds it (L1, CLAUDE.md §1).** The installer lives in the ops repository of a deployment (`submind-aws`, `submind-gcp`), not in this one, because the production address is deployment data. One command, using the person's existing GitHub access, builds the CLI from the factory commit the ops repository pins (so it matches what runs in production), links `factory`, and writes the deployment's address, and its sign-in method when the ops repository knows it, into the CLI's settings (`factory config set`). Re-running it updates to the then-pinned commit. It never overwrites a different address without asking, and no link or message can set the address: only a command the person chose to run from the ops repository.
* **CL11 – The CLI works without the manager (CL3, CL4, CL6, CL7).** Everything the manager can read or do, the person can also ask for directly: as slash commands in the conversation (`/agents`, `/agent <id>`, `/spend`, `/budgets`, `/runs`, `/approvals`, `/ledger`, `/pause <id>`, `/budget <agent> <amount> daily|monthly`, and the rest of the tool catalog) and as non-interactive subcommands that take `--json` for scripts. Commands and the manager use one tool catalog, so they have the same role filtering, the same confirmation before any change, the same factory calls made as the person, and the same ledgering; a command can never do what the manager could not. A command prints what the factory returned, with no model between (so a figure is the factory's own, CL7), hides values under secret-looking names (CL6), and offers only what the person's roles allow (CL3). Outside a terminal a command that changes state is refused unless `--yes` is given. With no manager admitted, or one that cannot answer (no policy, over budget, a model route not granted), free text says so and lists the commands instead of failing. Commands add no logic the console lacks: they render, they do not decide. The manager remains for plain-language questions, explanation and multi-step requests.


### 6.18 Agent Configuration and the Shared Runtime (Dale's settled design note and task instruction, 2026-10-23; each rule takes effect when its task lands)
Every agent created under this structure runs the same program. What makes an agent unique is its configuration, held by the factory: its **soul** (the persona and instructions), the **skills** it has adopted (the only place custom code lives), its **roles** and the access each role has, and its **memory archetype** (AM1), beside the model, policy and budget the factory already holds. This continues SK3 and SK4: the configuration record already holds an agent's pinned source, adopted skills, policy and owners, and every change to it is versioned and ledgered. Existing agents keep running from their repositories, unchanged; nothing in this section migrates, rebuilds or modifies one, and the design note's later step of migrating them is not part of this work. **Amendments:** where a rule below differs from an older invariant it says so and names the task that makes it true; until then the older text holds. **Not verified when this was drafted** (design note): whether the loop is identical line for line across existing agents, and whether `gatekeeper-ingress` can post interactive messages (GAP-196 records what was found).

* **CF1 – The factory holds the agent's configuration, and the factory's copy wins.** An agent's soul, roles (with the access each allows or denies per skill, E12) and memory settings are part of its versioned configuration record (SK3), as an optional `agent` section. The section is created in one of two ways only: when an agent is created from a configuration (CF6, TSK-270), which is the seed, or when an owner or admin sets it explicitly for an agent that has none. No commit and no registration ever creates it, overwrites it or changes it, so an agent registered from a repository keeps today's behaviour until someone sets its section; nothing here seeds, migrates or rebuilds an existing agent. Every change creates a new version recording who, when and why, and is ledgered with a content hash (never the soul text). For an agent with the section, changing the soul, the roles or the memory settings needs no pull request and no image build; the roles take effect at the Bouncer and the gatekeeper-egress at once (E12), while the soul and the memory settings take effect when the agent runs on the shared runtime (RT1), because a repository agent reads its own copies. Changing the archetype of an agent that already has a notebook takes effect at its next wake, deletes nothing, and the runtime opens the notebook by AM3's rules. *Amends E12(1) (an admin reviews the roles at admission) and AM1 (the archetype is in the cartridge and changing it is a commit) for agents with the section (pending TSK-264); an agent without it is unchanged.* The soul, the role definitions and the memory settings are elevated configuration (CF4). A role never creates reach: it only decides which people may use what the agent's policy already grants (E7, E12), so defining roles never grants a route, a credential or a model. An agent can never change its own soul, role definitions or memory settings, never asks for such a change, and never approves one; the only thing an agent may ask for is a role assignment (CF2). The factory stores and hashes the soul as opaque text and never reads it to decide anything.
* **CF2 – Owners assign people to roles; an agent may only ask.** An agent's owner may assign an existing identity to a role the agent defines, on that agent only (an admin may as well; `Owner` is never assigned, E12). An agent may *request* an assignment; the factory accepts the request only when the run's verified requester is the agent's owner, and the owner approves it (CF4). The agent can never assign a role, approve a request or change its own roles, which keeps E12's guarantee that the agent cannot change the answer on access. For an agent with a configuration section a role may name only skills the agent has adopted, so its adopted skills stay the ceiling. *Amends E12(2), where only an admin assigns roles (pending TSK-265). Applies to existing agents too: an owner may assign a person to a role the agent's cartridge declares.*
* **CF3 – A configuration moves as one bundle.** An owner or an admin can export an agent's whole configuration (soul, role and access definitions, memory settings, adopted skills with their pinned versions, policy). The bundle holds no secret, no person and no memory (AM5: the factory never opens a notebook). An agent with no configuration section can be exported only as a read-only record of its source pin, adopted skills and policy (its soul and roles are in its repository); such a bundle is refused by import, because importing it would register a new agent from a repository (CF6). A bundle with a configuration section can be imported into any factory as a new agent: the importer becomes its owner and the import is an elevated change (CF4). Import validates the bundle against the target factory's catalog and approved skills, creates nothing that is not allowed, and queues for an admin whatever E7, SK1 and SK3 reserve to one (the bundle's policy, which is a request and never applied by import, and any skill version not already approved there). An import never grants.
* **CF4 – Approval happens where the person is, and the agent never carries it.** Held actions (E9) and every elevated configuration change need approval, and a chat approval is as good as any other: there are no classes of change and no channel-trust settings, because if chat were not good enough for an action it would not be good enough at all. The elevated changes, each with its approver, are the single list: a held action (E9): the verified requester, or an owner only when the run has no requesting user (as the Bouncer does today, so an owner never approves what is done in another person's name), or an admin or approver as today; the soul, the memory settings, the agent's role definitions, a role assignment, and an import: an owner of the agent, or the verified requester when they are the owner (an owner who makes the change in a signed-in console or API session is both its requester and its approver, and the ledger records both); a grant or any other policy change, a public skill version, a public adoption, and a private skill re-adopted after a revocation (SK6): an admin (an owner, as owner, never approves a grant to their own agent; an admin who also owns an agent acts as admin, as E7 allows today); a private skill version and its automatic adoption: the owner of its owner agent (CF5). (1) The factory asks the approver in the channel they are using (a console modal, Discord buttons, a link for web or email); each channel needs a small adapter, and `gatekeeper-ingress` is their home. (2) `gatekeeper-ingress` intercepts the reply before the agent sees it; the decision is a button or a code tied to the approval id and to the approver's linked identity, so anyone who can see the code cannot use it, never free text routed to the model; the agent never sees, relays or answers an approval. (3) The factory renders the preview from its stored copy of the request; the agent's description is never trusted (GAP-161). (4) An approval is given once and unchanged (E9); a pending approval expires. (5) Only the person who may approve that change can approve it, checked against the identity link (the Bouncer's data); another person's click, and a click from an identity with no link, does nothing and is ledgered as refused. (6) The ledger records the approver, the channel and the time before the action is allowed, and a rejection or an expiry is ledgered too.
* **CF5 – Who approves a skill version.** *Amends SK1, SK3 and E9's admin review of declared holds, for private skills (pending TSK-271); this weakens that review on purpose, by Dale's instruction (2026-10-23), and is bounded as below.* A private skill version that passes validation (no secrets, no provider SDKs, tests pass, and the other checks, SK1) is approved by the owner of its owner agent, who may also have registered it; a public skill version is approved by a factory admin. The owner's approval includes the version's declared holds. The bounds: a skill reaches only routes the admin has already granted the agent (E7, E8), an admin may change any grant's hold at any time, and an admin sets every grant. A private skill re-adopted after a revocation (SK6) is still approved by an admin; its automatic adoption with a new version follows the owner's version approval (SK3). Approving a skill version grants nothing (SK2): any grant it needs (a route, a credential, a model) is a policy change, an elevated change that needs an admin's approval (CF4). Skills carried inline by existing agents are private skills of those agents (SK8), so this applies to them once registered.
* **CF6 – New agents are created under the configuration structure.** A new agent is created from a configuration and runs on the shared runtime (RT1); the factory refuses to register a new agent id from a repository (Dale, task instruction 2026-10-23: new agents can only be created under the new configuration structure). An id counts as existing if the factory has ever registered it and has not purged it: a retired or reinstated agent keeps its repository flow, and an id purged for good is new. *Amends L3 and L6 for new agents (pending TSK-270): the repository admission flow and the starter template remain for agents already registered; staff agents an operator admits from source (L2, such as the factory manager) are exempt.* An agent already registered keeps its repository, its image and its admission flow unchanged, including a new commit and a redeploy. A new agent needs no repository; its image is built only when it adopts skills (SK4).
* **RT1 – One shared runtime runs every new agent.** The runtime is one program that, for each wake, opens memory by the agent's archetype (AM1 to AM5), builds the prompt from the soul, the memory and the message, calls the model through the factory model API (M1), runs any tool or skill action the model asks for and repeats, replies on the channel the turn came from (AM2), saves memory, reports, and sleeps. A cron or scheduled run uses the same loop; only its caller differs (the person who made the schedule, or the system). It is versioned and pinned per agent, and an upgrade is staged, never forced. Each agent runs in its own container with its own notebook, and the runtime alone opens it, inside that container, so the factory never reads a notebook or a soul to decide anything, and the runtime is agent-side equipment under `runtimes/`, not kernel (`packages/*`), so the Donna Test holds: an agent's reasoning, memory and conversation are never factory code. AM5 is unchanged: the factory never opens a notebook, and the runtime answers the console's conversation request once for every agent.
* **RT2 – No extension points.** The loop is fixed. Agents differ by soul, skills, roles and memory archetype. Behaviour that does not fit those is built as a skill. **Skills are Python only; that is the current implementation** (skill checks run only for Python today, `docs/SKILLS.md`), and other languages are a future update that this rule does not add.
